Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time

| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,

   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"

title: FakeCaptcha / ClickFix - Clipboard-Injected PowerShell from Explorer
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d06
status: experimental
description: |
    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks
    user to press Win+R and paste a payload. The result is
    powershell/mshta launched directly from explorer.exe with iex /
    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/
author: Clankerusecase

    category: process_creation
    product: windows

    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\explorer.exe'

            - 'wget '

Miasma Worm Hits Microsoft Again: Azure Functions Action and 72 Other Repositories Disabled After Supply Chain Attack Targeting AI Coding Agents

StepSecurity
2026-06-09 12 use cases 4 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

On June 5, 2026, the Miasma worm campaign reached Microsoft's Azure GitHub organizations. GitHub disabled 73 repositories across four Microsoft GitHub organizations after a malicious commit was pushed to the Azure/durabletask repository using a previously compromised contributor account. The attack planted configuration files that execute a credential-harvesting payload when a developer opens the repository in Claude Code, Gemini CLI, Cursor, or VS Code.

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Suspicious commit pattern: '[skip ci]' with backdated timestamp adding only IDE config files · Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] node.exe spawned by Code/Cursor/Claude/Gemini executing .github/setup.js · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) · PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Miasma Worm Hits Microsoft Again: Azure Functions Action and 72 Other Repositori
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Malicious AI coding-agent hook configs written to repo (.claude/.gemini/.cursor/.vscode) · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Outbound DNS / HTTP to Miasma C2 (git-service.com / m-kosche.com) · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Malicious AI coding-agent hook configs written to repo (.claude/.gemini/.cursor/.vscode) AIInstallation High
Hunts the Miasma worm's signature artifact: simultaneous creation of SessionStart hooks and folderOpen tasks in .claude/settings.json, .gemini/settings.json, .cursor/rules/setup.mdc, and .vscode/tasks.json — all pointing at node .github/setup.js. This is the precise file-set planted in the June 5 2026 Azure/durabletask commit 5f456b8. Rationale: The five planted-file paths are verbatim from the article's 'Five Planted Files, Four Attack Vectors' section. Requiring >=2 co-occurrence per host suppresses single legitimate .vscode/tasks.json edits while keeping near-zero FP on the Miasma file-set. Cross-checked against: • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/002/ • https://github.com/step-security/supply-chain-firewall
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1546
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer scaffolding a new repo with VS Code tasks.json AND committing a .vscode workspace file at the same time
  • Engineering platform team rolling out a corporate .cursor/rules/ template across repos
  • Repo containing legitimate .github/setup.js for project bootstrapping (rare; size + obfuscation differentiates)
  • Renovate/Dependabot edits to .vscode/tasks.json without other paired files (will not trigger >=2 threshold)
  • Developer copying a template repo locally that legitimately includes both .claude and .vscode dirs
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("git.exe","Code.exe","Cursor.exe") and array_length(ArtifactsSeen) == 1 and ArtifactsSeen has "vscode-folderopen")
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: pull the InitiatingProcess (typically git.exe / Code.exe checkout) and inspect the .github/setup.js size and entropy; the Miasma payload is exactly 4,643,745 bytes single-line obfuscated JS. Pivot to DeviceProcessEvents for any node.exe child within 60s. Step 2 — Contain: isolate the host, kill any node setup.js process tree, force-revoke the developer's GitHub PAT / OAuth tokens, cloud CLI tokens (aws, az, gcloud), and rotate any .npmrc / .pypirc / kubeconfig secrets that the harvester targets. Step 3 — Hunt: search the org for any repo containing the five planted paths via GitHub code-search API; check for outbound DNS to check.git-service.com / t.m-kosche.com / git-service.com from any developer endpoint in the past 30 days.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where FolderPath has_any (@"\.claude\settings.json", @"\.gemini\settings.json", @"\.cursor\rules\setup.mdc", @"\.vscode\tasks.json", @"\.github\setup.js")
| extend Artifact = case(
    FolderPath has @"\.claude\settings.json", "claude-hook",
    FolderPath has @"\.gemini\settings.json", "gemini-hook",
    FolderPath has @"\.cursor\rules\setup.mdc", "cursor-prompt-injection",
    FolderPath has @"\.vscode\tasks.json", "vscode-folderopen",
    FolderPath has @"\.github\setup.js", "miasma-payload", "other")
| summarize ArtifactsSeen = make_set(Artifact), Paths = make_set(FolderPath), FirstSeen = min(Timestamp) by DeviceId, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| where array_length(ArtifactsSeen) >= 2  // co-occurrence of >=2 of the 5 planted files
| order by FirstSeen desc

| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileCreate")
| where TargetFilePath has_any (@"\.claude\settings.json", @"\.gemini\settings.json", @"\.cursor\rules\setup.mdc", @"\.vscode\tasks.json", @"\.github\setup.js")
| extend Artifact = case(
    TargetFilePath has @"\.claude\settings.json", "claude-hook",
    TargetFilePath has @"\.gemini\settings.json", "gemini-hook",
    TargetFilePath has @"\.cursor\rules\setup.mdc", "cursor-prompt-injection",
    TargetFilePath has @"\.vscode\tasks.json", "vscode-folderopen",
    TargetFilePath has @"\.github\setup.js", "miasma-payload", "other")
| summarize ArtifactsSeen = make_set(Artifact), Paths = make_set(TargetFilePath), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated) by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName
| where array_length(ArtifactsSeen) >= 2
| order by FirstSeen desc

node.exe spawned by Code/Cursor/Claude/Gemini executing .github/setup.js AIExploitation High
Hunts the Miasma worm's automatic execution chain: VS Code, Cursor, Claude Code, or Gemini CLI launching node.exe with .github/setup.js as the script argument. This is the runtime detonation produced when a developer simply opens a repo containing the planted hook configs. Rationale: Article quotes the exact command 'node .github/setup.js' invoked by all four planted configs (Claude SessionStart, Gemini SessionStart, Cursor prompt injection, VS Code folderOpen task). Anchoring on parent IDE + node.exe + setup.js path is high-fidelity because legitimate projects rarely store bootstrap scripts under .github/. Cross-checked against: • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1546/ • https://code.visualstudio.com/docs/editor/tasks#_run-behavior
ATT&CKT1059.007 T1546 T1204.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment

  • Security researcher analysing the Miasma sample in a sandbox (whitelist sandbox host IDs)

Suspicious commit pattern: '[skip ci]' with backdated timestamp adding only IDE config files AIDelivery Medium
Hunts the Miasma commit fingerprint: a single push where 5 files added / 0 source files modified, commit message ends with '[skip ci]', author date backdated to a pre-2024 timestamp, and the added paths include AI-agent config dirs. Detects via GitHub Audit Log shipped to Sentinel/Defender via CloudAppEvents or to Datadog via the GitHub integration. Rationale: Article details the exact commit fingerprint: commit 5f456b8 with message 'Switched DataConverter to OrchestrationContext [skip ci]', 5 files added / 0 source files modified, backdated to 2020-03-09T15:59:47Z (six years before push). The conjunction of [skip ci] + backdated author date + added IDE-config paths is the Miasma operator signature. Cross-checked against: • https://docs.github.com/en/enterprise-cloud@latest/admin/monitoring-activity-in-your-enterprise/reviewing-audit-logs-for-your-enterprise
ATT&CKT1195.001 T1078.004
Data sourcesChange.All_Changes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate maintainer importing an old Git history with [skip ci] in a merge commit
  • Repository migration from another VCS preserving original author dates pre-2024
  • CI maintenance commit adding .vscode/tasks.json with [skip ci] (will not match >=2 of the IDE paths usually)
  • Bot commits (Renovate/Dependabot) with skip-ci that touch tasks.json
  • Developer rebasing old branches with preserved author timestamps
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: pull the actor account from the audit event, check its recent activity for token regeneration / SSH key additions / new OAuth grants; correlate against the May 19 PyPI durabletask incident actor. Step 2 — Contain: suspend the GitHub account, revoke all PATs and OAuth grants, force-rotate any GitHub App / Action secrets the account had access to, and revert the commit from any affected default branches. Step 3 — Hunt: GitHub code-search the org for any other repos containing the five planted paths or the commit message 'Switched DataConverter to OrchestrationContext'; query GitHub Audit Log for the same actor across all owned orgs for [skip ci] commits adding files outside src trees.
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where Application == "GitHub"
| where ActionType in ("Repositories.Push","RepositoriesPush","git.push")
| extend Raw = tostring(RawEventData)
| where Raw has "[skip ci]"
| where Raw has_any (".claude/settings.json",".gemini/settings.json",".cursor/rules/setup.mdc",".vscode/tasks.json",".github/setup.js")
| extend AuthorDate = extract(@"author[^\d]+(\d{4})-\d{2}-\d{2}", 1, Raw)
| where isnotempty(AuthorDate) and toint(AuthorDate) < 2024
| project Timestamp, AccountDisplayName, AccountObjectId, IPAddress, ObjectName, AuthorDate, ActionType
| order by Timestamp desc

ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

title: FakeCaptcha / ClickFix - Clipboard-Injected PowerShell from Explorer
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d06
status: experimental
description: |
    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks
    user to press Win+R and paste a payload. The result is
    powershell/mshta launched directly from explorer.exe with iex /
    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1204.002
    - attack.execution

    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\explorer.exe'
            - '\RuntimeBroker.exe'

        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'

        CommandLine|contains:
            - 'iex'
            - 'invoke-expression'
            - 'frombase64'
            - 'downloadstring'
            - 'hxxp'

            - 'wget '

    - IT-driven scripted automation that admins paste-launch

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["069ac1dc7f7649b76bc72a11ac700f373804bfd81dab7e561157b703999f44ce", "3de04fe2a76262743ed089efa7115f4508619838e77d60b9a1aab8b20d2cc8bf", "85f54c089d78ebfb101454ec934c767065a342a43c9ee1beac8430cdd3b2086f", "c0b094e46842260936d4b97ce63e4539b99a3eae48b736798c700217c52569dc", "3a9db5ba0c8cd4c91e91717df6b1a141fc1e0fbc0558b5a78d7f5c23f5b2a150", "d630397de8b01af0f6f5cf4463da91b17f28195a2c50c8f3f38ad9f7873fdb8e", "633c8410ee0413ca4b090a19c30b20c03f31598c25247c484846fa34c1df5b64", "ef641e956f91d501b748085996303c96a64d67f63bfeef0dda175e5aa19cca90"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

We include indicators of activity and mitigations for PAN-OS vulnerability CVE-2026-0257. The post Threat Brief: Active Exploitation of PAN-OS CVE-2026-0257 appeared first on Unit 42 .

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] GlobalProtect successful login from CVE-2026-0257 pre-PoC exploitation IPs · [LLM] GlobalProtect connection with PoC-spoofed host-ID, MAC, or device name · [LLM] GlobalProtect connection with PoC client fingerprint: Win10 Pro 64-bit + empty d · PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → RMM tool installed by non-IT user — remote-access utility for hands-on-keyboard
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement
GlobalProtect successful login from CVE-2026-0257 pre-PoC exploitation IPs AIExploitation Medium
Hunts successful GlobalProtect portal/gateway sessions sourced from the nine IPs Unit 42 attributed to pre-PoC (before 2026-05-29) exploitation of the PAN-OS authentication bypass CVE-2026-0257. Any successful tunnel from these origins is a high-confidence foothold indicator. Rationale: Uses the nine source IPs Unit 42 explicitly attributed to pre-PoC (pre-2026-05-29) exploitation of CVE-2026-0257 against GlobalProtect portals/gateways. A successful gateway-connected event from any of these IPs is a direct foothold indicator. Falcon/CloudWatch empty because GlobalProtect VPN logs do not ship to FDR or CloudWatch Logs natively. Cross-checked against: • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/active-exploitation-of-pan-os-cve-2026-0257/
ATT&CKT1190 T1133
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate user behind a shared NAT/proxy that happens to overlap an IOC IP (uncommon for these specific hosters but possible)
  • Penetration testers / red team using one of the listed VPS providers (Frantech/BuyVM/Cogent ranges) for authorised assessments
  • Tor-exit overlap (23.128.228.6 is historically Tor-adjacent) for a user with a sanctioned anonymisation policy
  • Threat-intel feed back-population replaying these IPs into syslog after the fact
  • Recycled cloud IP no longer owned by the original adversary that a legitimate roaming user now uses
Suppression filter
| where SourceUserName !in~ ("redteam-svc","pentest-acct") and not (SourceIP == "23.128.228.6" and SourceUserName has "tor-sanctioned")
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: confirm the GlobalProtect log is a successful gateway-connected (not just portal-auth-fail), pull the assigned tunnel IP, host-id, machine_name, and src_user. Cross-check src_user against IdentityInfo and recent legitimate VPN history. Step 2 — Contain: terminate the GlobalProtect session via panorama (request global-protect-gateway client-logout), disable the associated user account, block the source IP at the perimeter, and force-revoke active SAML/refresh tokens for that user. Step 3 — Hunt: pivot on the tunnel-assigned internal IP across DeviceNetworkEvents and SecurityEvent 4624/4625 for the next 24h, look for lateral movement, SMB/WMI/WinRM probes, and any other gateway-connected events from the same host-id or PoC fingerprint described in the companion UCs.

GlobalProtect connection with PoC-spoofed host-ID, MAC, or device name AIExploitation High
Alerts on successful GlobalProtect gateway-connected events whose host-id or machine name matches the placeholder MACs (aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff, 00:11:22:33:44:55) or generic device names (WINDOWS-LAPTOP-001, DESKTOP-GP01, GP-CLIENT) hard-coded by the public PoC for CVE-2026-0257. These identifiers should not appear in any legitimate corporate VPN client. Rationale: Targets the exact five placeholder identifiers Unit 42 published: two patently-fake MACs (aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff is the canonical example MAC; 00:11:22:33:44:55 is a sequence-pattern placeholder) and three generic Windows device names baked into the PoC. Defender query is intentionally a corroboration baseline against DeviceInfo — if those device names never legitimately exist in the estate, any VPN log referencing them is definitively spoofed. Cross-checked against: • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/active-exploitation-of-pan-os-cve-2026-0257/
ATT&CKT1078 T1556 T1133
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Lab/test VPN client built with a placeholder MAC for QA
  • Vendor demo box temporarily named DESKTOP-GP01 during a Palo Alto Networks proof-of-value
  • Out-of-the-box Windows image not yet renamed (default DESKTOP-* pattern) connecting from a remote contractor
  • Internal red team replaying the PoC for authorised validation after disclosure
  • Long-tail of legacy MDM-unenrolled machines whose hostnames match the GP-CLIENT generic placeholder
Suppression filter
| where SourceUserName !in~ ("redteam-svc","qa-vpntest","sales-eng-demo")
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: pull the full GlobalProtect connection record (src_ip, src_user, host_id, machine_name, assigned_ip, client_version). Cross-reference DeviceInfo / asset inventory: does any owned asset legitimately use this hostname or MAC? Check whether the src_user has connected from this combo before. Step 2 — Contain: force-logout the GP session, disable the user account, block the source IP at the perimeter ACL, and isolate the tunnel-assigned internal IP from sensitive segments. Step 3 — Hunt: search the last 30 days of GP logs for the same host-id/MAC/hostname across other usernames (credential reuse), and pivot on the tunnel-assigned IP for SMB/RDP/WinRM/AD enumeration on internal hosts.
DeviceInfo
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| summarize LastSeen = max(Timestamp), DeviceCount = dcount(DeviceId) by DeviceName
| where DeviceName in~ ("WINDOWS-LAPTOP-001","DESKTOP-GP01","GP-CLIENT")
| project DeviceName, LastSeen, DeviceCount
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where DeviceVendor =~ "Palo Alto Networks"
| where DeviceProduct has_any ("GlobalProtect", "PAN-OS")
| where Activity has_any ("gateway-connected", "GlobalProtect connected", "portal-auth-succ")
| where Message has_any ("aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff", "00:11:22:33:44:55", "WINDOWS-LAPTOP-001", "DESKTOP-GP01", "GP-CLIENT")
   or SourceHostName in~ ("WINDOWS-LAPTOP-001","DESKTOP-GP01","GP-CLIENT")
   or SourceMACAddress in~ ("aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff","00:11:22:33:44:55")
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, SourceUserName, SourceHostName, SourceMACAddress, Activity, Message
| order by TimeGenerated desc

GlobalProtect connection with PoC client fingerprint: Win10 Pro 64-bit + empty d AIExploitation Medium
Hunts post-PoC-release GlobalProtect gateway-connected events whose endpoint_os_version field equals 'Microsoft Windows 10 Pro 64-bit' AND whose source_user_info.domain field is empty — the hard-coded client fingerprint left in the public PoC for CVE-2026-0257. Domain-joined corporate endpoints almost never present an empty domain. Rationale: Targets the exact two hard-coded PoC client-configuration values Unit 42 published for post-2026-05-29 hunting: endpoint_os_version literal 'Microsoft Windows 10 Pro 64-bit' AND empty source_user_info.domain. Sentinel regex uses a verbatim @-string so backslashes do not collide with JSON/KQL string escaping (fix for prior validation error). Defender query is an environment baseline — local-only Win10 Pro hosts that would plausibly emit an empty domain. Cross-checked against: • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/active-exploitation-of-pan-os-cve-2026-0257/

Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate BYOD / workgroup (non-domain-joined) Win10 Pro 64-bit users connecting to GlobalProtect with split-tunnel personal devices
  • Newly-imaged Win10 Pro laptop that has not yet been domain-joined when first GP connection is made
  • Contractor / consultant endpoints connecting from their own workgroup machines
  • QA / test rigs running stock Win10 Pro images without AD enrolment
  • Home-lab roaming users whose corporate device was rebuilt and lost domain trust
Suppression filter
| where SourceUserName !in (dynamic(["contractor-svc","byod-pilot-user"])) and SourceIP !in (dynamic(["<known-byod-egress-cidr>"]))
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: confirm the src_user is one that SHOULD only connect from a domain-joined device per corporate policy; check device-posture-check / HIP report for the same session — a legitimate corp endpoint would report domain membership. Step 2 — Contain: if user is on the domain-joined-only policy, force-logout the GP session, revoke the user's tokens, and require step-up MFA + device re-enrolment before the next login. Step 3 — Hunt: pivot on the tunnel-assigned internal IP for the next 24h across DeviceNetworkEvents and SecurityEvent 4624/4625; cross-correlate with the IP-IOC UC and the spoofed-host-id UC to find overlap; review whether other users from the same src_ip exhibit the same fingerprint.
DeviceInfo
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where OSPlatform =~ "Windows10" and OSVersion has "Pro"
| where isempty(LoggedOnUsers) or LoggedOnUsers !has "domain"
| summarize LastSeen = max(Timestamp) by DeviceId, DeviceName, OSPlatform, OSVersion, JoinType
| where JoinType !in~ ("AzureAD","Hybrid","Domain")
| project LastSeen, DeviceName, OSPlatform, OSVersion, JoinType
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DeviceVendor =~ "Palo Alto Networks"
| where DeviceProduct has_any ("GlobalProtect", "PAN-OS")
| where Activity has_any ("gateway-connected", "GlobalProtect connected")
| where Message has "Microsoft Windows 10 Pro 64-bit"
| where Message matches regex @"(?i)source_user_info\.domain\s*[:=]\s*(?:,|;|$|\s)"
   or Message matches regex @"(?i)domain\s*[:=]\s*(?:,|;|$|\s)"
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, SourceUserName, SourceHostName, DestinationIP, Activity, Message
| order by TimeGenerated desc
source:pan_os (@evt.name:gateway-connected OR @evt.name:"GlobalProtect connected") @endpoint_os_version:"Microsoft Windows 10 Pro 64-bit" (-@source_user_info.domain:* OR @source_user_info.domain:"")
APPLICATION
`pan_globalprotect` (event_id="gateway-connected" OR event_id="GlobalProtect connected" OR log_subtype="gateway-connect")
    endpoint_os_version="Microsoft Windows 10 Pro 64-bit"
    (source_user_info.domain="" OR NOT source_user_info.domain=*)
| stats earliest(_time) as firstTime latest(_time) as lastTime values(src_ip) as src_ips values(host_id) as host_ids values(machine_name) as machines count by src_user endpoint_os_version
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| where count >= 1
Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-0257")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-0257")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Wait, binding.gyp Can Do What? Exploring npm's Weirdest Build System

Aikido
2026-06-09 11 use cases 5 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

Deep dive into binding.gyp, the often overlooked npm build file that can execute malicious code at install time through shell expansions, sandbox escapes, and compiler hijacking. Category: Vulnerabilities & Threats

ATT&CKT1027 T1176 T1190 T1195.002 T1555.003
SHA256ef641e956f91d501… 288f26c2eadcb1a7… e3dbe63aded45278… ceff7c51d70832c3…

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Miasma-tainted package install: binding.gyp dropped into known-compromised npm package paths
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Wait, binding.gyp Can Do What? Exploring npm's Weirdest Build System
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Miasma Phantom Gyp: python.exe (gyp parser) spawning node index.js during npm install · [LLM] Miasma payload SHA256 hash hit (published Phantom Gyp IOCs) · Suspicious browser extension installation · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs · Crypto-wallet file/keystore access by non-wallet process
Miasma Phantom Gyp: python.exe (gyp parser) spawning node index.js during npm install AIInstallation High
Detects the core Miasma v2 abuse where node-gyp's Python parser executes a GYP command-expansion (<!(...)>) embedded in binding.gyp, spawning the credential-stealer payload as a child of python.exe rather than via package.json lifecycle hooks. Hunts the specific parent→child shape the worm produces during dependency install. Rationale: Anchored on the exact process tree the article describes: node-gyp invokes Python (gyp.py) to parse binding.gyp, and the embedded <!(node index.js > /dev/null 2>&1 && echo stub.c) command spawns node.exe with index.js as a direct child of python.exe — a parent→child shape that legitimate native add-on builds never produce (those go python→cl.exe/link.exe/cmake.exe). Allow-list of real toolchain children suppresses normal node-gyp activity. Cross-checked Semgrep, Endor Labs, StepSecurity write-ups confirming the python→node payload chain. Cross-checked against: • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2026/miasma-v2-self-spreading-npm-worm-now-uses-malicious-bindinggyp-file-and-compromises-57-packages/ • https://www.endorlabs.com/learn/malicious-payload-in-ai-sdk-ollama-npm-package • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/binding-gyp-npm-supply-chain-attack-spreads-like-worm • https://corgea.com/research/miasma-phantom-gyp-npm-worm-vapi-ai-sdk-ollama-june-2026
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.006 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate packages (e.g. node-pre-gyp, node-sass forks) where binding.gyp uses <!(node -e '...') to probe Node/Python versions at configure time
  • Developers running 'node-gyp rebuild --verbose' manually during native-addon debugging on a developer workstation
  • CI agents building Electron / better-sqlite3 / canvas / sharp where the configure step invokes node helpers (rare but possible)
  • Internal tooling that wraps node-gyp with custom Python pre-processors for cross-compilation
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("node-pre-gyp","--target_platform","--target_arch") and ProcessCommandLine has "-e" and ProcessCommandLine has "process.version")
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: pull the full process tree (npm.cmd → node.exe → python.exe → matching child). Inspect the binding.gyp at the parent's working directory (look for <!(...), <!@(...), or pymod_do_main tokens); review the child's command line for 'index.js' or sandbox-escape Python literals. Confirm which npm package owns the node_modules subdirectory the binding.gyp came from. Step 2 — Contain: isolate the host from network, revoke any npm/GitHub/AWS/GCP/Azure/Vault tokens that resided on it (Miasma harvests all of these), force-rotate the developer's npm 2FA recovery codes, and pause CI runners using the same npm cache. Step 3 — Hunt: pivot on InitiatingProcessAccountObjectId across DeviceFileEvents for reads of ~/.aws/credentials, ~/.ssh/id_rsa, ~/.npmrc, ~/.docker/config.json, ~/.config/gh/hosts.yml in the same window; check DeviceNetworkEvents for outbound to api.github.com / *.workers.dev from that node.exe PID; review npm audit log and GitHub audit log for any package republicatio
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("python.exe","python3.exe","python","python3")
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("gyp.py","node-gyp","binding.gyp","gyp_main")
| where FileName !in~ ("cl.exe","link.exe","cmake.exe","msbuild.exe","gcc.exe","g++.exe","clang.exe","clang++.exe","make.exe","nmake.exe","ld.exe","tracker.exe","rc.exe","cvtres.exe","mt.exe","conhost.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("node.exe","node","cmd.exe","sh","bash","dash","curl.exe","wget.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
   or ProcessCommandLine has_any ("index.js","__subclasses__","catch_warnings","echo stub.c","/dev/null 2>&1","__import__('os')","pymod_do_main")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath has_any ("node_modules","npm-cache","_npx","yarn","pnpm") or InitiatingProcessParentFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.cmd","npx.cmd","yarn.cmd","pnpm.cmd")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName matches regex @"(?i)[\\/]python3?(\.exe)?$"
| where ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("gyp.py","node-gyp","binding.gyp","gyp_main")
| where not(ActingProcessName matches regex @"(?i)[\\/](cl|link|cmake|msbuild|gcc|g\+\+|clang|clang\+\+|make|nmake|ld|tracker|rc|cvtres|mt|conhost)\.exe$")
| where ActingProcessName matches regex @"(?i)[\\/](node|cmd|sh|bash|dash|curl|wget|powershell|pwsh)(\.exe)?$"
   or ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("index.js","__subclasses__","catch_warnings","echo stub.c","/dev/null 2>&1","__import__('os')","pymod_do_main")
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, SHA256

id: 7f3b6c2a-d4e1-4a01-9c5e-3a4f1c8e7b22
description: Detects node-gyp's Python parser (gyp.py) spawning unexpected child processes during npm install, indicative of GYP command-expansion abuse used by the Miasma v2 / Phantom Gyp worm (June 2026)

  - https://semgrep.dev/blog/2026/miasma-v2-self-spreading-npm-worm-now-uses-malicious-bindinggyp-file-and-compromises-57-packages/

date: 2026/06/09

logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  parent_python_gyp:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\python.exe'
      - '\python3.exe'
    ParentCommandLine|contains:
      - 'gyp.py'
      - 'node-gyp'
      - 'binding.gyp'
  suspicious_child_image:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\node.exe'
      - '\cmd.exe'
      - '\sh.exe'
      - '\bash.exe'
      - '\curl.exe'
      - '\wget.exe'
      - '\powershell.exe'
      - '\pwsh.exe'
  suspicious_child_cmdline:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'index.js'
      - '__subclasses__'
      - 'catch_warnings'
      - 'echo stub.c'
      - "__import__('os')"
      - 'pymod_do_main'
  filter_compilers:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\cl.exe'
      - '\link.exe'
      - '\cmake.exe'
      - '\msbuild.exe'
      - '\gcc.exe'
      - '\clang.exe'
      - '\make.exe'
      - '\nmake.exe'
  condition: parent_python_gyp and (suspicious_child_image or suspicious_child_cmdline) and not filter_compilers
falsepositives:
  - Legitimate packages whose binding.gyp uses <!(node ...) for non-malicious build-time configuration (e.g. node-pre-gyp version probing)
  - Developers manually invoking node-gyp rebuild for native add-on debugging
level: high
source:(windows.sysmon OR windows.security) @EventID:(1 OR 4688) (@ParentImage:(*\\python.exe OR *\\Python.exe OR *\\PYTHON.EXE OR *\\python3.exe) OR @ParentCommandLine:(*gyp.py* OR *node-gyp* OR *binding.gyp*)) AND (@Image:(*\\node.exe OR *\\Node.exe OR *\\NODE.EXE OR *\\cmd.exe OR *\\CMD.EXE OR *\\powershell.exe OR *\\pwsh.exe OR *\\curl.exe OR *\\wget.exe) OR @CommandLine:(*index.js* OR *__subclasses__* OR *catch_warnings* OR *"echo stub.c"* OR *pymod_do_main* OR *__import__* )) AND -@Image:(*\\cl.exe OR *\\link.exe OR *\\cmake.exe OR *\\msbuild.exe OR *\\gcc.exe OR *\\clang.exe OR *\\make.exe OR *\\nmake.exe)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ParentBaseFileName=/^python3?\.exe$/i
| ParentCommandLine=/(gyp\.py|node-gyp|binding\.gyp|gyp_main)/i
| (ImageFileName=/\\(node|cmd|sh|bash|dash|curl|wget|powershell|pwsh)\.exe$/i OR CommandLine=/(index\.js|__subclasses__|catch_warnings|echo stub\.c|\/dev\/null 2>&1|__import__\('os'\)|pymod_do_main)/i)
| NOT ImageFileName=/\\(cl|link|cmake|msbuild|gcc|g\+\+|clang|clang\+\+|make|nmake|ld|tracker|rc|cvtres|mt)\.exe$/i
| UserName!=/\$$/
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ImageFileName, CommandLine, ParentCommandLine, SHA256HashData], function=count())

Miasma-tainted package install: binding.gyp dropped into known-compromised npm package paths AIDelivery High
File-event detection for binding.gyp files written into node_modules subdirectories of the publicly named Miasma v2 victims (@vapi-ai/server-sdk, ai-sdk-ollama, @redhat-cloud-services/*). These packages historically had no native add-on, so the mere presence of binding.gyp inside their node_modules path is the campaign signature. Rationale: The article explicitly names @vapi-ai/server-sdk (71k weekly downloads) and ai-sdk-ollama (31k weekly downloads) as victims and references the @redhat-cloud-services namespace; corroborating sources confirm these packages historically had no native code, so binding.gyp inside their tree is the campaign signature. Detection is path-anchored to those exact package names rather than generic binding.gyp activity. Cross-checked Semgrep, Chainguard, and Endor Labs. Cross-checked against: • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2026/miasma-v2-self-spreading-npm-worm-now-uses-malicious-bindinggyp-file-and-compromises-57-packages/ • https://www.chainguard.dev/unchained/chainguard-artifacts-safe-from-miasma-phantom-gyp-npm-attack • https://www.endorlabs.com/learn/malicious-payload-in-ai-sdk-ollama-npm-package
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1505.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A vendor-published clean (post-incident) version of one of these packages ships a legitimate binding.gyp — verify file hash against the npm registry tarball
  • Local file restore from backup containing an old node_modules tree
  • Forensic copy / IR engineer staging the malicious sample onto a sandbox host for analysis
  • A developer cloning the repo (not via npm) where a legitimate maintainer added a build-time helper in binding.gyp
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: read the dropped binding.gyp content (extract from disk or EDR file-capture) and confirm it contains <!(...), <!@(...), pymod_do_main, or Python sandbox-escape literals (__subclasses__, catch_warnings). Confirm the installed package version matches the IOC range (@vapi-ai/server-sdk >0.11.0, ai-sdk-ollama >0.13.0). Step 2 — Contain: stop any in-flight node-gyp rebuild PID, isolate the host, and treat ALL credentials reachable from the host as burned — npm tokens, GitHub PATs, AWS/GCP/Azure CLI configs, kubeconfigs, Vault tokens, .npmrc auth. Trigger automated rotation. Step 3 — Hunt: query all hosts and CI runners for the same package-path pattern in the last 14 days; check the maintainer's npm account for unauthorized package republications via npm audit logs; review GitHub org for newly created repos under the developer's account (Miasma uses GitHub for dead-drop exfil).
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where FileName =~ "binding.gyp"
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where FolderPath has_any (
    @"node_modules\@vapi-ai\server-sdk",
    "node_modules/@vapi-ai/server-sdk",
    @"node_modules\ai-sdk-ollama",
    "node_modules/ai-sdk-ollama",
    @"node_modules\@redhat-cloud-services",
    "node_modules/@redhat-cloud-services")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("git.exe","code.exe","explorer.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, FolderPath, FileName, FileSize, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessParentFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

    @"node_modules\@redhat-cloud-services",
    "node_modules/@redhat-cloud-services")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSize, TargetFileSHA256, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine

Miasma payload SHA256 hash hit (published Phantom Gyp IOCs) AIInstallation Medium
Hash-based match for the four SHA256s associated with the Miasma v2 Phantom Gyp payload set. Fires on either file write or process execution telemetry. Universal IOC sweep across endpoint estate. Rationale: Direct hash IOC sweep against the four SHA256s the article ships. Hash detections are non-negotiable for any campaign with published IOCs — universal, low-FP, and works across file-event and process-event surfaces. Coverage spans both DeviceFileEvents (catches the dropped artifact even if it never executes) and DeviceProcessEvents (catches execution attempts). Cross-checked against: • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2026/miasma-v2-self-spreading-npm-worm-now-uses-malicious-bindinggyp-file-and-compromises-57-packages/
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Forensic analyst or malware researcher intentionally placing the IOC sample on a sandbox / detonation host
  • Security tooling (e.g. EDR test harness, YARA-rule unit test fixtures) staging the hash for validation
  • Threat-intel platform pre-caching the file for hash-lookup tests
  • Inbound email or chat attachment carrying the sample for IR analysis (will hit Defender mail scan first)
Suppression filter
| where DeviceName !in ("SOC-SANDBOX-01","IR-WORKSTATION-01","MALWARE-LAB-01")
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: confirm the host is not a sanctioned forensic / sandbox / IR-analyst workstation by checking the device tag and the user's role in IdentityInfo. Pull the full file path and parent process for the hash hit; if it executed (DeviceProcessEvents row), the host is presumed compromised. Step 2 — Contain: isolate the device immediately via Live Response / Defender machine action, suspend the user's npm / GitHub / cloud sessions, kill the offending PID. Treat all secrets reachable from the host as burned and force rotation (npm tokens, GitHub PATs, AWS keys, .npmrc auth, kubeconfig, Vault tokens). Step 3 — Hunt: pivot on InitiatingProcessAccountObjectId and SHA256 across the full estate for the last 30 days; check whether any package on npm under the user's maintainer account was published in the same window (Miasma republishes from victim hosts); review GitHub audit log for new repos under the user's account in the last 7 days (potential dead-drop exfil endpoint).
let MiasmaHashes = dynamic([
  "ef641e956f91d501b748085996303c96a64d67f63bfeef0dda175e5aa19cca90",
  "288f26c2eadcb1a7923fe376d16f5404216cce15d9fc162a4a78574dc7df399a",
  "e3dbe63aded45278f49c4746ab938ed9472b36def79b43e2dd2d7eff014481d1",
  "ceff7c51d70832c3ec8dd2744b606a23b3c924ef664ae23439b9b742ea154108"
]);
union
  ( DeviceFileEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where SHA256 in (MiasmaHashes)
    | project Timestamp, Source="FileEvent", DeviceName, AccountName=InitiatingProcessAccountName, Path=FolderPath, FileName, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine ),
  ( DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where SHA256 in (MiasmaHashes)
    | project Timestamp, Source="ProcessEvent", DeviceName, AccountName, Path=FolderPath, FileName, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName=InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine=ProcessCommandLine )
| order by Timestamp desc

Node child of node-gyp/python making outbound to GitHub dead-drop or anomalous web service during install AIexfil Medium
Catches the Miasma exfiltration step: after the binding.gyp command-expansion fires, the spawned node.exe (running index.js) beacons cloud credentials and CI tokens out to attacker-controlled GitHub repositories or webhook services. Pivots on the rare combination of node.exe with a node-gyp/python ancestor making outbound TCP to api.github.com, raw.githubusercontent.com, or first-seen external hosts. Rationale: Anchors on the article's described post-execution behavior (credential exfil to attacker-controlled dead-drops) with the parent-process chain unique to binding.gyp abuse. Corroborating sources (Endor Labs, Morphisec, OX Security) confirm Miasma exfils to GitHub dead-drop repos and uses Workers/webhook bouncers; this detection requires both the gyp ancestry AND the suspicious destination to fire, keeping FPs manageable while catching the campaign-specific exfil shape. Cross-checked against: • https://www.endorlabs.com/learn/malicious-payload-in-ai-sdk-ollama-npm-package • https://www.morphisec.com/blog/its-in-your-ai-assistant-now-shai-hulud-wave-3-and-the-miasma-worm-targeting-npm/ • https://www.ox.security/blog/600000-monthly-downloads-affected-miasma-supply-chain-attack-is-back-on-npm/
ATT&CKT1567.001 T1552.001 T1041
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate npm packages whose postinstall hook fetches assets from raw.githubusercontent.com (e.g. node-pre-gyp downloading prebuilt binaries) — but those typically run as direct child of npm, not via python.exe (gyp) chain
  • node-gyp packages that pull source tarballs from GitHub during configure step (rare; node-pre-gyp uses HTTP downloader)
  • CI runners that genuinely push to api.github.com via node-based GitHub API client called from a build hook
  • Cloudflare Workers used by an org's internal build accelerator service
Suppression filter
| where not (ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("node-pre-gyp","prebuild-install") and DstHostname has "objects.githubusercontent.com")
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: pull the spawning binding.gyp content and the node process's full argv; identify which npm package owns the source path. Check whether the GitHub destination repo is org-controlled (legitimate) or an externally owned account (likely dead-drop). Step 2 — Contain: kill the node.exe PID, isolate the device, and immediately rotate every credential reachable from the host — focus first on the developer's GitHub PAT (worm publishes via it), npm publish token, AWS keys in ~/.aws/credentials, .npmrc auth, kubeconfig, Vault tokens, GCP/Azure CLI cache. Step 3 — Hunt: query GitHub audit log for new repository creations under the developer's user account in the last 24h (Miasma creates ephemeral dead-drop repos); search ImNetworkSession for any other node.exe egress to the same destination from CI runners in the last 7 days; check npm registry for unauthorized package publications under the dev's maintainer accounts.
let gyp_node_children = DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
    | where FileName =~ "node.exe"
    | where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("python.exe","python3.exe","cmd.exe","sh","bash")
    | where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("gyp.py","binding.gyp","node-gyp")
        or InitiatingProcessParentFileName in~ ("python.exe","python3.exe")
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project ProcSpawnTime=Timestamp, DeviceId, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessId, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessCommandLine;
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "node.exe"
| where RemoteUrl has_any ("api.github.com","raw.githubusercontent.com","objects.githubusercontent.com",".workers.dev","webhook.site","ngrok.io","hooks.slack.com","discord.com/api/webhooks","transfer.sh","file.io","0x0.st")
   or RemoteIPType == "Public"
| join kind=inner gyp_node_children on DeviceId, $left.InitiatingProcessId == $right.ProcessId
| where Timestamp between (ProcSpawnTime .. ProcSpawnTime + 5m)
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort, ProcessCommandLine=InitiatingProcessCommandLine, GypParentCmd=InitiatingProcessCommandLine1
| order by Timestamp desc
let gyp_node_children = ImProcessCreate
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
    | where ActingProcessName matches regex @"(?i)[\\/]node(\.exe)?$"
    | where ParentProcessName matches regex @"(?i)[\\/](python3?|cmd|sh|bash)(\.exe)?$"
    | where ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("gyp.py","binding.gyp","node-gyp")
    | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
    | project ProcSpawnTime=TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActingProcessId, ParentProcessCommandLine;
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DstHostname has_any ("api.github.com","raw.githubusercontent.com","objects.githubusercontent.com",".workers.dev","webhook.site","ngrok.io","hooks.slack.com","discord.com","transfer.sh","file.io","0x0.st")
| join kind=inner gyp_node_children on DvcHostname
| where TimeGenerated between (ProcSpawnTime .. ProcSpawnTime + 5m)
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, SrcUsername, DstHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, NetworkBytes, ParentProcessCommandLine
source:(windows.sysmon OR crowdstrike) @EventID:3 @Image:(*\\node.exe OR *\\Node.exe OR *\\NODE.EXE) (@DestinationHostname:(*api.github.com* OR *raw.githubusercontent.com* OR *.workers.dev OR *webhook.site* OR *ngrok.io* OR *hooks.slack.com* OR *discord.com* OR *transfer.sh* OR *file.io* OR *0x0.st*))
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ImageFileName=/\\node\.exe$/i ParentBaseFileName=/^(python3?|cmd|sh|bash)\.exe$/i ParentCommandLine=/(gyp\.py|binding\.gyp|node-gyp)/i
| join({#event_simpleName=DnsRequest DomainName=/(api\.github\.com|raw\.githubusercontent\.com|objects\.githubusercontent\.com|\.workers\.dev$|webhook\.site|ngrok\.io|hooks\.slack\.com|discord\.com|transfer\.sh|file\.io|0x0\.st)/i}, field=aid, include=[DomainName, ContextImageFileName])
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, DomainName, CommandLine, ParentCommandLine], function=count())

title: Obfuscated / Encoded PowerShell Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d08
status: experimental
description: |
    PowerShell launched with -EncodedCommand, base64 strings, hidden
    window, no-profile, bypass policy, IEX / Invoke-Expression / Net
    .WebClient.DownloadString — combinations indicating evasion.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1027
    - attack.execution
    - attack.defense-evasion
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_image:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
    selection_args:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '-enc '
            - 'EncodedCommand'
            - 'FromBase64String'
            - 'Invoke-Expression'
            - 'IEX('
            - 'DownloadString'
            - 'Net.WebClient'
            - '-w hidden'
            - '-WindowStyle Hidden'
            - '-nop'
            - '-NoProfile'
            - '-ep bypass'
            - '-ExecutionPolicy Bypass'
    condition: selection_image and selection_args
falsepositives:
    - DevOps tooling that legitimately uses encoded command for
      cross-shell escaping
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*-enc *" OR Processes.process="*EncodedCommand*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64String*" OR Processes.process="*-nop*"
        OR Processes.process="*-w hidden*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*IEX(*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*Net.WebClient*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*-enc *" OR Processes.process="*EncodedCommand*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64String*" OR Processes.process="*-nop*"
        OR Processes.process="*-w hidden*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*IEX(*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*Net.WebClient*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |

    category: process_creation

        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'

            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'

        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'

      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["ef641e956f91d501b748085996303c96a64d67f63bfeef0dda175e5aa19cca90", "288f26c2eadcb1a7923fe376d16f5404216cce15d9fc162a4a78574dc7df399a", "e3dbe63aded45278f49c4746ab938ed9472b36def79b43e2dd2d7eff014481d1", "ceff7c51d70832c3ec8dd2744b606a23b3c924ef664ae23439b9b742ea154108"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

CISA KEV
2026-06-09 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

Google Chromium V8 out-of-bounds read and write vulnerability that could allow a remote attacker to execute arbitrary code inside a sandbox via a crafted HTML page. This vulnerability could affect multiple web browsers that utilize Chromium, including, but not limited to, Google Chrome, Microsoft Edge, and Opera. Vendor: Google, Product: Chromium V8. Federal patch due: 2026-06-23.

DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities

| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-11645")

| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-11645")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId

          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

nebula-mesh: API endpoints lack ownership checks, enabling cross-operator privilege escalation

nebula-mesh CVE-2026-47724 — cross-operator admin API key mint via POST /api/v1/operators/{id}/api-keys AIExploitation High
Hunts the exact endpoint abused in exploit chain A — handleCreateOperatorAPIKey lacks any admin/ownership check pre-v0.3.4, so any successful POST to /api/v1/operators/{id}/api-keys by a non-admin actor mints a fresh bearer for an arbitrary operator (including admins). On an unpatched server this single request is privilege escalation. Rationale: Endpoint path /api/v1/operators/{id}/api-keys and the handleCreateOperatorAPIKey function at internal/api/operators.go:118 are quoted verbatim from the GHSA — exploit chain A delivers an admin bearer in one curl with no ownership check pre-v0.3.4. Successful POST on this exact path is the signal. No external corroboration available — CVE freshly minted (2026-47724) and GHSA is the authoritative source.
ATT&CKT1068 T1078.003 T1190
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate admin provisioning API keys for a newly created operator via the admin UI
  • Automated key rotation cron job owned by the ops team hitting this endpoint on a schedule
  • IaC/Terraform provider running operator-bootstrap during deployment
  • First-time setup script that seeds the initial admin operator and its API keys
  • Internal API health check or integration test that creates and immediately deletes a key
Suppression filter
// Sentinel example — suppress known admin sources and automation user-agents
| where cIP !in ("10.10.0.5", "10.10.0.6")  // ops bastion IPs
| where csUserAgent !startswith "terraform-provider-nebula/"
| where csUserAgent !contains "nebula-keyrotate-cron"
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the bearer token's owning operator_id from the nebula-mesh app audit log for this request; if actor operator_id != target operator_id in the URL path, or actor is non-admin and target is an admin operator, treat as confirmed exploit. Capture the returned API key ID from the response body for forensics. 2) Contain: immediately revoke the newly minted bearer via DB-level UPDATE on operator_api_keys (do not use the API — attacker may still hold admin); rotate the source operator's key; force-upgrade the server to v0.3.4+. 3) Hunt: replay the last 30 days of /api/v1/operators/*/api-keys POSTs from the same source IP and bearer; pivot into /api/v1/hosts/*/reenroll, /api/v1/enroll, and /api/v1/networks/*/firewall calls within the same session window to scope blast radius.
// Primary: W3CIISLog when nebula-mesh is fronted by IIS/Azure App Gateway. Adapt cs* fields for AppServiceHTTPLogs or CommonSecurityLog (RequestURL/RequestMethod) as needed.
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where csMethod == "POST"
| where csUriStem matches regex @"^/api/v1/operators/[^/]+/api-keys/?$"
| where scStatus in (200, 201)
| extend TargetOperatorId = extract(@"/api/v1/operators/([^/]+)/api-keys", 1, csUriStem)
| extend CVE = "CVE-2026-47724"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, ClientIP=cIP, AuthUser=csUserName, csMethod, csUriStem, TargetOperatorId, scStatus, UserAgent=csUserAgent, CVE
| order by TimeGenerated desc
